id: "2440214"
title: "Chinese intelligence activity abroad"
---

# Chinese intelligence activity abroad

&#x20; The [Chinese Communist Party](7175.md) and [government of the People's Republic of China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_of_the_People's_Republic_of_China) is engaged in [espionage](10501.md) overseas, directed through diverse methods via the [Ministry of State Security](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_State_Security_of_the_People's_Republic_of_China) (MSS), the [Ministry of Public Security](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Public_Security_\(China\)) (MPS), the [United Front Work Department](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Front_Work_Department) (UFWD), [People's Liberation Army](66890.md) (PLA) via its [Intelligence Bureau of the Joint Staff Department](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intelligence_Bureau_of_the_Joint_Staff_Department), and numerous [front organizations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Front_organization) and [state-owned enterprises](204768.md). It employs a variety of tactics including [cyber espionage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyber_spying) to gain access to sensitive information remotely, [signals intelligence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Signals_intelligence), [human intelligence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_intelligence_\(intelligence_gathering\)) as well as [influence operations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_warfare) through [united front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_front_\(China\)) activity targeting [overseas Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Overseas_Chinese) communities and associations.[^cite_note-USCC2018-1]

The Chinese government is also engaged in [industrial espionage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industrial_espionage) aimed at gathering information and technology to bolster its economy, as well as [transnational repression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transnational_authoritarianism) of [dissidents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Chinese_dissidents) abroad such as supporters of the [Tibetan independence movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tibetan_independence_movement) and [Uyghurs](31783.md) as well as the [Taiwan independence movement](31156.md), the [Hong Kong independence movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong_independence_movement), [Falun Gong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falun_Gong), [pro-democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democracy_movements_of_China) activists, and other critics of the [Chinese Communist Party](7175.md) (CCP).[^cite_note-aw1-2][^cite_note-3][^cite_note-New_York_Times-4] The United States alleges that the degree of intelligence activity is unprecedented in its assertiveness and engagement in multiple host countries, particularly the United States, with economic damages estimated to run into the hundreds of billions according to the [Center for Strategic and International Studies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Center_for_Strategic_and_International_Studies).

## Modes of operation

Further information: [Cyberwarfare and China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyberwarfare_and_China)



Seal of the [Ministry of State Security (China)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_State_Security_\(China\))

It is believed that Chinese espionage is aimed at preserving China's national security through gaining commercial, technological, and military secrets.[^cite_note-5][^cite_note-6][^cite_note-7][^cite_note-8][^cite_note-9][^cite_note-10][^cite_note-11][^cite_note-12] The carriers of China's intelligence activities are diverse.[^cite_note-13][^cite_note-14][^cite_note-15] The use of non-traditional intelligence assets is codified in [Chinese law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Law_of_the_People's_Republic_of_China). Article 14 of China's 2017 [National Intelligence Law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Intelligence_Law_of_the_People's_Republic_of_China) mandates that Chinese intelligence agencies "may ask relevant institutions, organizations and citizens to provide necessary support, assistance and cooperation."[^cite_note-16] [Honey trapping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Honey_trapping) and [kompromat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kompromat) are also common tools of Chinese intelligence services.[^cite_note-17]

Much of the information available to the public about the Chinese intelligence services comes from defectors, whom the PRC accuses of lying to promote an anti-PRC agenda.[^cite_note-18][^cite_note-19][^cite_note-20][^cite_note-21] One known exception to this rule is the case of [Katrina Leung](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Katrina_Leung), who was accused of starting an affair with an [FBI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FBI) agent to gain sensitive documents from him. A U.S. judge dismissed all charges against her due to [prosecutorial misconduct](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prosecutorial_misconduct).[^cite_note-22]

The United States believes the Chinese military has been developing network technology to perform espionage on other nations. Several cases of computer intrusions suspected of Chinese involvement have been found in various countries, including Australia, New Zealand, Canada, France, Germany, the Netherlands, the United Kingdom, India and the United States.[^cite_note-23][^cite_note-24][^cite_note-25]

In the aftermath of the [Shadow Network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shadow_Network) computer espionage operation, security experts claimed "targeting Tibetan activists is a strong indicator of official Chinese government involvement" since private Chinese hackers pursue economic information only.[^cite_note-26] In 2009, Canadian researchers at the [Munk Center for International Studies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Munk_Center_for_International_Studies) at the University of Toronto examined the computers at the personal office of the [Dalai Lama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/14th_Dalai_Lama). Evidence led to the discovery of [GhostNet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GhostNet), a large cyber-spy network. Chinese hackers had gained access to computers possessed by government and private organizations in 103 countries, although researchers say there is no conclusive evidence China's government was behind it. Computers penetrated include those of the [Dalai Lama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dalai_Lama), [Tibetan exiles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tibetan_exile), organizations affiliated with the Dalai Lama in India, Brussels, London and New York, embassies, foreign ministries and other government offices, and focus was believed to be on the governments of [South Asian](21566765.md) and [Southeast Asian](28741.md) countries.[^cite_note-27][^cite_note-28][^cite_note-29] The same researchers discovered a second cyberspy network in 2010. They were able to see some of the stolen documents that included classified material about Indian missile systems, security in several [Indian states](375986.md), confidential embassy documents about India's relationships in [West Africa](67393.md), [Russia](25391.md) and the [Middle East](19323.md), NATO forces travel in [Afghanistan](737.md), and a years worth of the Dalai Lama's personal email. The "sophisticated" hackers were linked to universities in China. Beijing again denied involvement.[^cite_note-30][^cite_note-31] In 2019, Chinese hackers posing as *[The New York Times](30680.md)*, [Amnesty International](18947898.md) and other organization's reporters targeted the private office of the Dalai Lama, [Tibetan Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tibetan_Parliament) members, and Tibetan nongovernmental organizations, among others. [Facebook](7529378.md) and [Twitter](9988187.md) took down a large network of Chinese bots that was spreading disinformation about the [2019–20 Hong Kong protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019%E2%80%9320_Hong_Kong_protests) and a months long attack on Hong Kong media companies was traced to Chinese hackers.[^cite_note-New_York_Times-4][^cite_note-32]

[Facial recognition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Facial_recognition_system) and [surveillance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surveillance) [artificial intelligence](1164.md) (AI) technology developed inside China to identify [Uyghurs](31783.md), a [Muslim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim) minority,[^cite_note-33] is now used throughout China, and despite security [concerns over Chinese involvement in 5G wireless networks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Concerns_over_Chinese_involvement_in_5G_wireless_networks), is manufactured and exported worldwide by state owned [China National Electronics Import & Export](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China_National_Electronics_Import_&_Export_Corporation) and [Huawei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Huawei) to many countries, including Ecuador, Zimbabwe, Uzbekistan, Pakistan, Kenya, the United Arab Emirates, Venezuela, Bolivia, Angola and Germany.[^cite_note-34] American companies and universities such as [MIT](18879.md) and [Princeton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Princeton_University) are partnering with the [Rockefeller Foundation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rockefeller_Foundation) and the [California Public Employees' Retirement System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CalPERS) to fund Chinese surveillance and AI start-ups such as [Hikvision](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hikvision), [SenseTime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SenseTime) and [Megvii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Megvii), which sell less expensive versions of Chinese state developed artificial intelligence surveillance systems, although this is being curtailed somewhat due to the companies being declared national security threats and [human rights](13831.md) violators by the US, and [US-China trade concerns](57055272.md).[^cite_note-35][^cite_note-36][^cite_note-37][^cite_note-38]

In July 2020, in its annual report, [Germany](11867.md)'s domestic intelligence agency, the [Federal Office for the Protection of the Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Office_for_the_Protection_of_the_Constitution), warned consumers that personal data they provide to Chinese payment companies or other tech firms such as [Tencent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tencent), Alibaba and others, could end up in the hands of China's government.[^cite_note-39] In September 2020, a Chinese company, [Shenzhen Zhenhua Data Technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zhenhua_Data_leak) came under the scanner worldwide for its big data and data mining and integration capacities and intentions related to its use.[^cite_note-40] According to the information from the National Enterprise Credit Information Publicity System, which is run by [State Administration for Market Regulation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_Administration_for_Market_Regulation) in China, the shareholders of Zhenhua Data Information Technology Co., Ltd. are two natural persons and one general partnership enterprise whose partners are natural persons.[^cite_note-41] Wang Xuefeng, who is the chief executive and the shareholder of Zhenhua Data, has publicly boasted that he supports "hybrid warfare" through manipulation of public opinion and "psychological warfare".[^cite_note-42]

### Agencies

The primary agencies involved in deploying operatives overseas are the Ministry of State Security and the [Intelligence Bureau of the Joint Staff Department](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intelligence_Bureau_of_the_Joint_Staff_Department) of the [Central Military Commission](214345.md), both utilizing [state-owned enterprises](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State-owned_enterprises_of_China) and united front groups acting as front organizations for intelligence operatives disguised as legitimate employees.[^cite_note-43] The [Ministry of Public Security](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Public_Security_\(China\)) is also involved in domestic counter-intelligence and overseas capture of fugitives, dissidents and corruption suspects through activities such as [Operation Fox Hunt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Fox_Hunt). The United Front Work Department is responsible for conducting political influence operations leveraging overseas Chinese diaspora and local political and economic elites while providing cover for intelligence agents.[^cite_note-\:2-44][^cite_note-45][^cite_note-46]

[Xinhua News Agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xinhua_News_Agency) also collects and reports information on individuals and groups of interest for intelligence purposes.[^cite_note-47] Xinhua reporters file certain [internal reports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neican) (*[neican](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neican)*) to CCP leadership from secure rooms in some Chinese embassies and consulates.[^cite_note-48]

### Relationship with the United Front

Further information: [United front (China)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_front_\(China\)) and [Hidden front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hidden_front)

In 1939, [Zhou Enlai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zhou_Enlai) espoused "nestling intelligence within the [united front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Front_\(China\))" while also "using the united front to push forth intelligence."[^cite_note-\:2-44] According to Australian analyst [Alex Joske](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alex_Joske), "the united front system provides networks, cover and institutions that intelligence agencies use for their own purposes." Joske added that "united front networks are a golden opportunity for Party's spies because they represent groups of Party-aligned individuals who are relatively receptive to clandestine recruitment."[^cite_note-\:2-44]

In 2023, [Chen Wenqing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chen_Wenqing) of the CCP's [Central Political and Legal Affairs Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Political_and_Legal_Affairs_Commission) directed party [cadres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cadre_system_of_the_Chinese_Communist_Party) and committees at all levels to "attach great importance to, concern themselves with, and support covert front work."[^cite_note-86]

## Activity worldwide

### Africa

#### Ethiopia

See also: [2018 China-African Union espionage allegations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_China-African_Union_espionage_allegations)

In January 2018, *[Le Monde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Le_Monde)* reported that the headquarters of the [African Union](10634933.md), which had been constructed by the [China State Construction Engineering Corporation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China_State_Construction_Engineering_Corporation), had had its computer systems compromised between 2012 and 2017, with data from AU servers being forwarded to [Shanghai](27643.md).[^cite_note-LeMonde-87][^cite_note-88][^cite_note-FT-89] The building's computer system was subsequently removed and the AU refused a Chinese offer to configure the replacement system. *Le Monde* alleged that the AU had then covered up the hack to protect Chinese interests in the continent.[^cite_note-LeMonde-87][^cite_note-90]

China and the African Union have rejected the allegations.[^cite_note-91] Ethiopian Prime Minister [Hailemariam Desalegn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hailemariam_Desalegn) rejected the French media report, saying that he doesn't believe it.[^cite_note-92] [Moussa Faki Mahamat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moussa_Faki), head of the African Union Commission, said the allegations in the *Le Monde*'s report were false. "These are totally false allegations and I believe that we are completely disregarding them."[^cite_note-93]

In 2020, Japan's Computer Emergency Response Team (CERT) reported that a suspected Chinese hacking organization, "Bronze President," had hacked and extracted footage from the AU Headquarters' security cameras.[^cite_note-94]

#### South Africa

In 2007, the People's Republic of China dispatched two armed teams to break into the [Pelindaba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pelindaba) nuclear research centre to steal technology for a [pebble bed modular reactor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pebble_bed_modular_reactor), according to South Africa's [State Security Agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_Security_Agency_\(South_Africa\)). A guard at the reactor was shot in the chest during the break-in.[^cite_note-95]

### Asia

#### Cambodia

Since at least April 2017, TEMP.Periscope, an [advanced persistent threat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Advanced_persistent_threat) based in China, has been hacking Cambodian organizations related to the [2018 general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_Cambodian_general_election).[^cite_note-camfire-96] Targets included the National Election Commission, the [Ministry of Interior](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Interior_\(Cambodia\)), the [Ministry of Foreign Affairs and International Cooperation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Foreign_Affairs_and_International_Cooperation_\(Cambodia\)), the [Senate of Cambodia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Senate_of_Cambodia), and the [Ministry of Economy and Finance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Economy_and_Finance_\(Cambodia\)).[^cite_note-camfire-96] The APT engaged in [spear phishing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phishing#CITEREFrajput2023) against [Monovithya Kem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monovithya_Kem) of the [Cambodia National Rescue Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cambodia_National_Rescue_Party), sending messages which impersonated the [Cambodian League for the Promotion and Defense of Human Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cambodian_League_for_the_Promotion_and_Defense_of_Human_Rights).[^cite_note-camfire-96]

#### Hong Kong

Further information: [United front in Hong Kong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_front_in_Hong_Kong) and [Causeway Bay Books disappearances](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Causeway_Bay_Books_disappearances)

According to [pro-democracy](3291227.md) political groups,[^cite_note-97] China has been sending spies into [Hong Kong](13404.md) to harass dissidents and [Falun Gong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falun_Gong_in_Hong_Kong) practitioners. In 2012, according to *[Oriental Daily News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oriental_Daily_News)*, a Chinese security ministry official was arrested in Hong Kong on suspicion of acting as a double agent for the United States.[^cite_note-98]

In October and December 2015, five booksellers from [Causeway Bay Books](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Causeway_Bay_Books) disappeared as part of the [Causeway Bay Books disappearances](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Causeway_Bay_Books_disappearances). The five men, [Gui Minhai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gui_Minhai), Lee Bo, Lui Bo, Cheung Jiping, and [Lam Wing-kee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lam_Wing-kee), were allegedly kidnapped by [Chinese public security bureau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_security_bureau_\(China\)) officials from mainland China. These extrajudicial acts were in breach of Hong Kong's judicial independence and were a result of the men publishing and distributing books containing corruption and scandals related to the senior leadership of the Chinese Communist Party.[^cite_note-99]

#### India

The [Research and Analysis Wing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Research_and_Analysis_Wing) (R\&AW) believes that China is using dozens of study centers it has set up in Nepal near the Indian border in part to spy on India.[^cite_note-100][^cite_note-101]

In August 2011 a Chinese [research vessel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Research_vessel) disguised as a [fishing trawler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fishing_trawler) was detected off the coast of [Little Andaman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Little_Andaman), collecting data in a [geostrategically](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geostrategy) sensitive region.[^cite_note-102][^cite_note-103]

The "Luckycat" hacking campaign that targeted Japan and [Tibet](31516.md) also targeted India.[^cite_note-TrendMicro-104][^cite_note-105] A [Trojan horse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trojan_horse_\(computing\)) was inserted into a [Microsoft Word](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Microsoft_Word) file ostensibly about India's [ballistic missile defense](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ballistic_missile_defense) program, allowing for the [command and control](7092305.md) servers to connect and extract information. The attacks were subsequently traced back to a Chinese graduate student from [Sichuan](65185.md) and the Chinese government is suspected of planning the attacks.[^cite_note-NYT-106]

Chinese hackers linked to the Third Technical Department of the People's Liberation Army have launched extensive and sustained hacking campaigns against the [Central Tibetan Administration](54751.md), based in [Dharamshala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dharamshala).[^cite_note-MITJan10-107]

In 2018, PLA Navy deployed a [Type 815G ELINT ship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Type_815_spy_ship#Type_815G) in waters off [Andaman and Nicobar Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andaman_and_Nicobar_Islands) for two weeks, according to a report by Indian intelligence agencies.[^cite_note-108]

In March 2019, Indian intelligence agencies told news services that China was trying to spy on [Indian Navy](604522.md) bases located in [southern India](678583.md) and [Integrated Test Range](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Integrated_Test_Range) missile testing facility located at [Abdul Kalam Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdul_Kalam_Island). It was doing this by establishing Chinese business around these areas.[^cite_note-109]

The [Central Board of Direct Taxes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Board_of_Direct_Taxes) in August 2020 caught [Luo Sang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luo_Sang), a Chinese person for operating a [money laundering](19390.md) scheme. He reportedly employed people to gather information on the [Dalai Lama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dalai_Lama)'s movements while simultaneously keeping an eye on pro-Tibetan individuals in [Delhi](37756.md) and the [northeastern states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northeast_India).[^cite_note-110] In September 2020, [Delhi Police](8808263.md) arrested a journalist and accomplices under the [Official Secrets Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Official_Secrets_Act_\(India\)) for allegedly passing sensitive information to Chinese intelligence officers.[^cite_note-111][^cite_note-112]

On 3 March 2021, the Chinese hackers attacked the [Covaxin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Covaxin) and [Covishield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Covishield) units In India.[^cite_note-113] It is also being claimed that the hackers tried to create a national blackout in the country by breaching the electricity units.[^cite_note-114]

On 21 October 2022, [*The Tribune*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Tribune_\(India\)), an Indian English newspaper, reported that Delhi Police apprehended a Chinese female citizen hailing from Hainan province in China, and she was accused of assuming a false identity as a monk and allegedly engaging in "anti-national activities."[^cite_note-115]

In February 2023, Indian police detained a Chinese national accused of spying against India under the [Indian Penal Code](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Penal_Code) (IPC) section 121 (waging war against the government of India) and other IPC sections, after the Chinese national visited "key installations" in Delhi, India.[^cite_note-116]

On 10 May 2024, a Chinese national, was arrested by [Uttar Pradesh Police](14570210.md) and [Sashastra Seema Bal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sashastra_Seema_Bal) for [espionage](10501.md) activities along the [India-Nepal border](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/India%E2%80%93Nepal_border). Photographs of [Indian Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Army) installations and his trips to [Pakistan](23235.md) were found on his phone.[^cite_note-117]

Since China's state security laws demand enterprises to collaborate with intelligence work, India implemented software and hardware testing regulations for [closed-circuit television camera](4252697.md) manufacturers starting in April 2025.[^cite_note-118]

In December 2025, Hu Congtai, a 29-year-old Chinese national, was detained in [Jammu and Kashmir](61449812.md) for allegedly violating the terms of his tourist visa by travelling to restricted areas in [Ladakh](303611.md) and Kashmir. Authorities found that he visited strategically sensitive locations, stayed in unregistered accommodations, obtained a local SIM card, and conducted online searches related to [CRPF](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Reserve_Police_Force) deployments and [Article 370](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Article_370_of_the_Constitution_of_India), prompting security concerns.[^cite_note-119][^cite_note-120]

#### Indonesia

In January 2021, Indonesian fishermen reported findings of underwater drones or gliders. This finding is thought to be related to a glider belonging to China that was previously found by fishermen around the waters of [Selayar Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Selayar_Island), [South Sulawesi](389458.md). Foreign media have highlighted these findings. Most of them call this incident a secret Chinese mission in Indonesian waters. *[The Guardian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Guardian)*, in its Espionage rubric, details previously that a Chinese-made glider was also found by fishermen in [Tanjung Pinang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tanjung_Pinang), [Riau Islands](453745.md) in March 2019. Furthermore, the finding also occurred in [Masalembo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Masalembu_Islands), [East Java](320443.md) in January 2020.[^cite_note-121]

In September 2021, Chinese hackers have breached the internal networks of at least ten Indonesian government ministries and agencies, including computers from Indonesia's primary intelligence service, [Indonesian State Intelligence Agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_Intelligence_Agency_\(Indonesia\)) (BIN). The intrusion, discovered by Insikt Group, the threat research division of [Recorded Future](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recorded_Future), has been linked to Mustang Panda, a Chinese threat actor known for its cyber-espionage campaigns targeting the Southeast Asian region. But a spokesman of the Indonesia intelligence agency denies China hackers hacked into their computers.[^cite_note-122][^cite_note-123]

In July 2022, the [Indonesian Navy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indonesian_Navy) arrested six people, including three foreigners, two from Malaysia and one from China, on suspicion of being involved in espionage activities in North Sebatik, [North Kalimantan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Kalimantan) after they were found in possession of sensitive naval base photos at the shared island, according to Indonesian media reports.[^cite_note-124][^cite_note-\:11-125]

#### Japan

In 1976, Wang Yangran, a trader residing in Hong Kong, was arrested by the [Tokyo Metropolitan Police Department](30871408.md) on suspicion of violating the Foreign Exchange and Foreign Trade Act. Wang used Hong Kong as a base to collect information on advanced Japanese technology using connections with Chinese authorities. This incident revealed the reality that Hong Kong, then under British rule, functioned as a detour route (Hong Kong route) for goods and information to mainland China.[^cite_note-126]

In 2004, a clerk at the Consulate-General of Japan in Shanghai committed suicide. The clerk had been subjected to a honey trap and blackmail by Chinese public security authorities, who coerced him to provide diplomatic classified information. The clerk took his own life leaving a suicide note, preventing the leakage of information, but the incident exposed the reality of China's forceful recruitment operations targeting diplomats.[^cite_note-\:11-125][^cite_note-127]

In 2006, [Yamaha Motor Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yamaha_Motor_Company) was raided for violating the Foreign Exchange and Foreign Trade Act for attempting to illegally export industrial unmanned helicopters capable of military diversion to China. The export destinations included companies deeply connected to the [People's Liberation Army](66890.md).[^cite_note-128]

In March 2007, Yang Luchuan, a Chinese engineer at major automotive parts manufacturer [Denso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Denso), stole a large amount of drawings for automotive products by downloading and taking them out without permission.[^cite_note-\:10-129] Among these, about 280 types were related to state-of-the-art technology considered Denso's top secret; this Chinese engineer had returned to China multiple times during the period he was obtaining the company's data.[^cite_note-\:10-129]

According to a report by [Trend Micro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trend_Micro), the "Luckycat" hacker group is engaged in cyber-espionage on targets in Japan, India and Tibet. During the [2011 Tōhoku earthquake and tsunami](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011_T%C5%8Dhoku_earthquake_and_tsunami) and [nuclear meltdowns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011_Japanese_nuclear_accidents) at [Fukushima](11986472.md), the hackers inserted a [Trojan virus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trojan_virus) into [PDF](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PDF) attachments to emails being circulated containing information about radiation dosage measurements.[^cite_note-TrendMicro-104][^cite_note-130] Investigation into ownership of the command and control [servers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Computer_server) by Trend Micro and *The New York Times* linked the malware to Gu Kaiyuan, through [QQ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tencent_QQ) numbers and the alias "scuhkr".[^cite_note-NYT-106][^cite_note-131] Gu was a graduate student of the Information Security Institute of [Sichuan University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sichuan_University) in [Chengdu](170375.md) and wrote his master's thesis on [computer hacking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hacker_\(computer_security\)).[^cite_note-132] [James A. Lewis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Andrew_Lewis) of the [Center for Strategic and International Studies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Center_for_Strategic_and_International_Studies), believes the attacks were state-sponsored.[^cite_note-NYT-106] Starting in 2019, Chinese state hacking group, [MirrorFace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MirrorFace), commenced [cyberattacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyberwarfare_and_China) against Japanese governmental institutions, according to the country's [National Police Agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Police_Agency_\(Japan\)).[^cite_note-133]

Since 2010, cyberattacks against the Japanese manufacturing industry have intensified. There were a total of 42 cases since 2010, in which intellectual property such as manufacturing blueprints was stolen.[^cite_note-\:9-134]

In January 2011, a French executive at Renault took out technical confidential information regarding electric vehicles developed by Nissan Motor Corporation. The French newspaper Le Figaro reported on January 7, citing multiple sources, that "the final recipient of the leaked information is China".[^cite_note-135]

In 2012, Li Chunguang, a First Secretary at the Chinese Embassy in Japan, was charged with opening a bank account under a false identity. Li was alleged to be an officer of the General Staff Department of the People's Liberation Army and was suspected of collecting classified information in the agriculture, forestry, and fisheries sectors and engaging in political maneuvering. Li refused police requests to appear for questioning and returned to China using diplomatic immunity.[^cite_note-136]

In 2014, a former employee of Nissan Motor Corporation illegally took out a large amount of technical data including trade secrets. He was arrested for violating the Unfair Competition Prevention Act, highlighting the seriousness of technology leakage in the automotive sector, Japan's leading industry.[^cite_note-137]

From September 2016 to April 2017, a large-scale cyberattack occurred by the hacker group "Tick" related to the People's Liberation Army. The Tokyo Metropolitan Police Department referred a male system engineer, a member of the Chinese Communist Party, to prosecutors for signing a contract for the rental server used in the attack under a false name.[^cite_note-138]

In 2017, an overseas police station under the Public Security Bureau of [Nantong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nantong), Jiangsu Province, was established without Japan's consent. Commemorative photos taken at the time of establishment show the Nantong Municipal Party Committee Secretary and a counselor from the Chinese Embassy, suggesting it was established with official embassy recognition.[^cite_note-139]

In a cyberattack on Mitsubishi Electric discovered in 2020, the involvement of Chinese hacker groups "Tick" and "BlackTech" was suspected, and defense-related information was leaked.[^cite_note-140] In addition, activities by the Chinese state hacking group [MirrorFace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MirrorFace) have been confirmed.[^cite_note-141]

According to the [Tokyo Metropolitan Police Department](30871408.md), the group is believed to have started cyberattacks against Japanese government agencies in 2019.[^cite_note-142]

In 2020, the Ministry of Foreign Affairs' system for exchanging official telegrams containing diplomatic secrets was subjected to a Chinese cyberattack, resulting in a large-scale information leak.[^cite_note-143] U.S. National Security Agency (NSA) Director Paul Nakasone and others visited Japan to meet with high-ranking Japanese government officials, and working-level officials from Japan and the U.S. discussed the response.[^cite_note-144]

Regarding the persistent attacks on the [Japan Aerospace Exploration Agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japan_Aerospace_Exploration_Agency) (JAXA) from 2023 to 2024, the National Police Agency and the National center of Incident readiness and Strategy for Cybersecurity (NISC) identified them as being committed by MirrorFace and issued an unprecedented public attribution.[^cite_note-145]

In 2023, Quan Hengdao, a Chinese national and senior researcher at the [National Institute of Advanced Industrial Science and Technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Institute_of_Advanced_Industrial_Science_and_Technology) (AIST), was arrested for leaking fluorine compound synthesis technology to a Chinese company.[^cite_note-146] Quan participated in China's "Thousand Talents Plan" and was in a state of "dual affiliation," concurrently serving as a professor at Beijing Institute of Technology.

In 2024, the Tokyo Metropolitan Police Department conducted a search of a building in Akihabara believed to be a Chinese public security overseas base.[^cite_note-147]

#### Kazakhstan

On 19 February 2019, Kazakh counterintelligence officers arrested Konstantin Syroyezhkin, a former [KGB](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KGB) agent, in [Almaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Almaty), on charges of passing classified documents to Chinese spies.[^cite_note-148] China-linked hackers have also targeted entities in Kazakhstan.[^cite_note-Cyberscoop_2020-149]

#### Malaysia

In 2020 Chinese hackers were implicated in the hacking of a Malaysian government official. The attacks were attributed to APT40.[^cite_note-150]

#### Philippines

Further information: [China–Philippines relations § National security concerns in the Philippines](33734272.md#National_security_concerns_in_the_Philippines)

See also: [2025 China–Philippines espionage cases](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_China%E2%80%93Philippines_espionage_cases)

[FireEye](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FireEye) President Travis Reese has stated that the Chinese-sponsored Conference Crew, founded in 2016, has engaged in cyber-espionage against the Philippines, targeting diplomatic and national security information.[^cite_note-151]

In 2020, Facebook took down a network that was part of a Chinese disinformation campaign against the Philippines. The campaign used false profiles to influence public opinion, particularly related to politics.[^cite_note-152]

Following a murder in [Makati City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Makati) in which two Chinese [PLA](66890.md) IDs were recovered,[^cite_note-153] Philippines Senator [Panfilo Lacson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panfilo_Lacson) claimed he had received information that between 2,000 and 3,000 Chinese [PLAN](24918.md) (People's Liberation Army Navy) members were in the Philippines.[^cite_note-154] Replying on Twitter, the Chinese Embassy in Manila said the Senator was "testing the intelligence of the Philippine people", to which Lacson responded saying his information is still worth looking into, while the [Armed Forces of the Philippines](23448.md) said it is validating the information as a "matter of serious concern."[^cite_note-155]

In November 2023, the Chinese embassy in the Philippines denied allegations made by [Rafael Alunan III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rafael_Alunan_III) of having [sleeper cells](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sleeper_cells) in the country after the [Philippine National Police](1313055.md) and [National Bureau of Investigation](2489044.md) arrested Chinese nationals for [illegal possession of firearms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illegal_firearm_trade_in_the_Philippines).[^cite_note-156]

In 2024, former [Bamban](2144429.md) mayor [Alice Guo](76882460.md) was removed from office over questions regarding her citizenship as well her involvement in the [Philippine Offshore Gaming Operator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_Offshore_Gaming_Operator) (POGO) in her town.[^cite_note-157][^cite_note-self-confessed-158] [She Zhijiang](73181369.md) a self-confessed spy detained in Thailand named Guo as a fellow spy.[^cite_note-159] Wang Fugui a cellmate of She also said that Guo's 2022 mayoral campaign was "arranged by Chinese state security".[^cite_note-160] Guo's birth citizenship was eventually voided in October 2025.[^cite_note-voided-161] *[The Economist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Economist)* wrote in July 2026 that Guo was unlikely to be a spy, noting that the claims have not been backed by persuasive evidence and that the Chinese government has a negative view of scammers, who often target Chinese citizens. The newspaper added that the fact that Guo escaped to Southeast Asia rather than China was further evidence that she was not a spy.[^cite_note-162]

[Deng Yuanqing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deng_Yuanqing) was arrested in January 2025 over allegations of mapping sensitive data involving military sites in [Luzon](69375.md).[^cite_note-163]

#### Singapore

[Huang Jing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Huang_Jing_\(academic\)) (黄靖), an academic at the [Lee Kuan Yew School of Public Policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lee_Kuan_Yew_School_of_Public_Policy), was expelled from Singapore in 2017, reportedly for working as an [agent of influence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agent_of_influence) for Chinese intelligence services.[^cite_note-164][^cite_note-165]

[SingHealth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SingHealth) medical data was hacked by suspected Chinese hackers around July 2018.[^cite_note-166]

#### South Korea

FireEye claims that two hacking operations tied to the Chinese military, dubbed Tonto Team and Stone Panda/[APT10](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/APT10), attempted to hack the South Korean [Ministry of Foreign Affairs](11533941.md) and other targets related to the deployment of [THAAD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/THAAD).[^cite_note-ArsROK-167][^cite_note-168] China has reportedly been engaged in economic espionage against South Korean technology companies, including [Samsung Electronics](46445426.md) and [SK Hynix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SK_Hynix).[^cite_note-ktchina-169] In July 2024, government agencies from eight nations, including South Korea's [National Intelligence Service](991544.md), released a joint advisory on [APT40](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/APT40).[^cite_note-\:22-170][^cite_note-\:32-171] In 2025, [SK Telecom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SK_Telecom) was reported to be affected by attacks from China-based hacking group Red Menshen.[^cite_note-172][^cite_note-173] In June 2026, two Chinese nationals were sentenced to prison for illegally filming the [Busan Naval Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Busan_Naval_Base) with drones.[^cite_note-174] In August 2026, South Korean authorities arrested two former Chinese military servicemen over suspected spying on U.S. military communications.[^cite_note-175]

#### Sri Lanka

In 2010, [Jayalalithaa Jayaram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jayalalithaa_Jayaram) – head of the [All India Anna Dravida Munnetra Kazhagam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/All_India_Anna_Dravida_Munnetra_Kazhagam) – stated that Chinese workers, working in parts of the country devastated by the [Sri Lankan Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sri_Lankan_civil_war) were infiltrated with Chinese spies on surveillance missions targeted at India.[^cite_note-176] In May 2019, Sri Lankan authorities caught the former chief of [Military intelligence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_Intelligence_Corps_\(Sri_Lanka\)) for allegedly acting as a Chinese mole and trying to obstruct a probe by Indian and American agencies into the [Easter bombings](60550092.md).[^cite_note-177]

#### Taiwan

See also: [United front in Taiwan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_front_in_Taiwan)

Taiwan and China regularly accuse each other of spying.[^cite_note-178][^cite_note-179]

Presidential aide Wang Jen-ping was found in 2009 to have sold nearly 100 confidential documents to China since 2007; Military intelligence officer Lo Chi-cheng was found to have been acting as a double agent in 2010 for China since 2007; Maj. Gen. [Lo Hsien-che](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lo_Hsien-che), electronic communications and information bureau chief during the administration of former President Chen Shui-bian, has been suspected of selling military secrets to mainland China since 2004.[^cite_note-180]

In 2007 the [Ministry of Justice Investigation Bureau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Justice_Investigation_Bureau) stated that 500 gigabyte Maxtor Basics Personal Storage 3200 [hard drives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hard_drives) produced by [Seagate Technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seagate_Technology) and manufactured in Thailand may have been modified by a Chinese [subcontractor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subcontractor) and shipped with the Virus.Win32.AutoRun.ah virus.[^cite_note-181][^cite_note-182] As many as 1,800 drives sold in the Netherlands and Taiwan after August 2007 were reportedly infected with the virus, which scanned for [passwords](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Passwords) for products such as *[World of Warcraft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_of_Warcraft)* and QQ and uploading them to a website in Beijing.[^cite_note-183]

Zhou Hongxu (周泓旭), a graduate of [National Chengchi University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Chengchi_University)'s [MBA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MBA) program, has been accused of attempting to recruit an official from the [Ministry of Foreign Affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Foreign_Affairs_\(Taiwan\)) to provide intelligence to China.[^cite_note-ReutersZhou-184][^cite_note-JapanZhou-185] Zhou was reportedly instructed by China's [Taiwan Affairs Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taiwan_Affairs_Office) to enroll in the university to make friends and develop a spy ring.[^cite_note-JapanZhou-185] Zhou reportedly solicited classified documents, offering trips to [Japan](15573.md) and [United States dollars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_dollars) in payment.[^cite_note-JapanZhou-185][^cite_note-186]

In October 2020 it was revealed that Chinese hackers had compromised the largest [job bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Job_bank) in Taiwan, hacking the information of over five million people.[^cite_note-187]

On 11 December 2020, the Ministry of Justice Investigation Bureau (MJIB) caught three Taiwanese who worked for Chinese intelligence. They were spreading propaganda about how Taiwan and the US were trying to overthrow the Thai monarchy, supporting the democracy protesters. The case is important because it is Taiwan's first Internet-related national security case, which the bureau investigated. Secondly, it is the first time Taiwan has documented that China has successfully recruited Taiwanese to work as paid online agents of its cyber army.[^cite_note-188][^cite_note-189] This disinformation operation also tried to falsely portray the [Milk Tea Alliance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Milk_Tea_Alliance) as a [color revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Color_revolution) style American government plot.[^cite_note-190]

Chinese spy balloons have overflown Taiwan.[^cite_note-191]

In August 2022, during [Speaker of the House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Speaker_of_the_United_States_House_of_Representatives) [Nancy Pelosi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nancy_Pelosi)'s visit to Taiwan, a TV screen at a Taiwan Railway Ministration (TRA) station displayed a message referring to Pelosi as an "old witch" in simplified Chinese characters (official characters in mainland China), while TV screens at multiple 7-Elevens began referring to her as a "warmonger."[^cite_note-\:4-192][^cite_note-193] China was suspected of hacking the TRA signs and Taiwan 7-Eleven to mock Pelosi.[^cite_note-\:4-192]

In March 2023, Taiwan prosecutors charged two former officials with violating the national security law by organizing meetings between former senior military officers and Chinese intelligence personnel.[^cite_note-\:5-194] Retired rear admiral Hsia Fu-hsiang and former lawmaker Lo Chih-ming began their espionage after being recruited by the Chinese military and [United Front Work Department](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Front_Work_Department).[^cite_note-195] Each faces up to five years in jail if convicted.[^cite_note-\:5-194]

In August 2023, Taiwan's [Ministry of National Defense](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_National_Defense_\(Republic_of_China\)) detained a lieutenant colonel, Hsieh, for leaking military secrets to China. Hsieh and several others were suspected of having been recruited by China to provide China with national defense secrets and other information.[^cite_note-\:3-196] Hsieh is also suspected of developing a spy organization of current and retired military personnel to collect intelligence for China.[^cite_note-\:3-196]

In April 2026, Chu Cheng-Chi, a former aide and DPP primary candidate for Taipei city council, was indicted for allegedly leaking sensitive legislative information to China.[^cite_note-197]

In August 2026, German journalist [Jürgen Kremb](https://de.wikipedia.org/wiki/J%C3%BCrgen_Kremb) stated that he was followed by a female Chinese Intelligence operative on [Alishan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alishan,_Chiayi) in 2015, which the MJIB confirmed.[^cite_note-198]

##### Military espionage

Further information: [Intelligence Bureau of the Joint Staff Department](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intelligence_Bureau_of_the_Joint_Staff_Department) and [Anti-Infiltration Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Infiltration_Act)

In May 2017, Major Wang Hung-ju, a retired [military police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_police) officer assigned to the [National Security Bureau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Security_Bureau_\(Taiwan\)), was indicted on charges of spying for the People's Republic of China.[^cite_note-199]

Army Major General Hsieh Chia-kang, deputy commander of Matsu Defense Command, has been accused of providing intelligence to China, having been recruited by retired army colonel Hsin Peng-sheng.[^cite_note-200]

In January 2018, it was reported that the Taipei District Prosecutor's Office is investigating if classified information regarding the [Airborne Special Service Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Airborne_Special_Service_Company) was passed on to Zhou Hongxu (周泓旭), who was already convicted for violating the National Security Act.[^cite_note-201] In March 2018, a retired colonel was charged with breaching the National Security Act by the Kaohsiung District Prosecutors' Office, which alleged that the colonel shared classified personal information and planned to develop a [spy ring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spy_ring) in Taiwan.[^cite_note-202] In April 2018, Hung Chin-hsi (洪金錫), a [Macau](19068.md)-born businessman, was accused of developing a spy ring in the [Ministry of Justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Justice_\(Taiwan\)), on behalf of China.[^cite_note-203] Captain Zhen Xiaojiang (鎮小江) was convicted in 2015 of recruiting Taiwanese military officers as part of a spy ring on behalf of China, including Army Major-General Hsu Nai-chuan (許乃權). Zhen sent intelligence regarding Taiwan's radar installations and [Dassault Mirage 2000](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dassault_Mirage_2000) [fighters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fourth-generation_jet_fighter) to China. He was deported to Hong Kong in July 2018.[^cite_note-hk-204]

In 2022, Taipei District Prosecutors Office charged retired major general Chien and retired lieutenant colonel Wei for developing a spy network for China. They were accused of working for a Hong Konger named Tse, who would visit Taiwan to recruit retired officers and reach out to those on active duty. Chien and Wei tried unsuccessfully to recruit [Chang Che-ping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chang_Che-ping), who was a deputy minister at the ROC Ministry of National Defense before serving as a strategic adviser to President [Tsai Ing-wen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tsai_Ing-wen). Chang was probed as a defendant last year but later renamed as a witness.[^cite_note-205]

From January 2022 to June 2024, the Taiwanese government reported over 1,700 instances of Chinese intelligence trying to recruit Taiwanese military personnel.[^cite_note-206]

##### Economic espionage

Further information: [Industrial espionage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industrial_espionage) and [Allegations of intellectual property infringement by China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allegations_of_intellectual_property_infringement_by_China)



Integrated circuit made in Taiwan

*[The Wall Street Journal](173070.md)* reported that Taiwan has been "ground zero" for economic espionage related to its [integrated circuit fabrication industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semiconductor_industry_in_Taiwan).[^cite_note-wsjroc-207] In a review of ten prosecutions for technology-related intellectual property infringement in Taiwan, *WSJ* found that nine of those cases involved technology transfer to China.[^cite_note-wsjroc-207] An employee of Nanya Technology Corp. allegedly stole designs for [dynamic random-access memory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dynamic_random-access_memory) (DRAM) on behalf of [Tsinghua Holdings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tsinghua_Holdings).[^cite_note-wsjroc-207] Hsu Chih-Peng, an engineer for [Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing Co.](463574.md), allegedly stole microchip designs after being solicited by the Chinese government-owned Shanghai Huali Microelectronics Corporation.[^cite_note-wsjroc-207]

According to Taiwanese prosecutors, engineer Wang Yongming (on behalf of Fujian Jinhua Integrated Circuit) engaged in espionage to steal [Micron Technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Micron_Technology) microchip designs via the Taiwanese company UMC.[^cite_note-heist-208] Micron alleges that UMC hired Micron's engineers, asking them to steal files on advanced memory chips.[^cite_note-wsjroc-207][^cite_note-heist-208] The files Wang allegedly stole were said to be production secrets, including testing procedures related to metallization, and the DR25nmS design protocol.[^cite_note-wsjroc-207]

#### Vietnam

According to the security research firm FireEye, Chinese hackers are suspected of targeting Vietnamese government and corporate targets.[^cite_note-FEV-209] The hacking group, designated Conimes, [phished](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phishing) the targets and delivered malware via a pre-2012 version of Microsoft Word.[^cite_note-FEV-209][^cite_note-210]

### Europe

According to the cyber-security firm Area 1, hackers working for the [People's Liberation Army Strategic Support Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Liberation_Army_Strategic_Support_Force) compromised the [COREU](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COREU) network used for communication by the [European Union](9317.md) (EU), allowing it to access thousands of low-classified documents and [diplomatic cables](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diplomatic_cable).[^cite_note-A1-211]

According to a 2019 report released by the [European External Action Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_External_Action_Service), an estimated 250 MSS spies were operating in Brussels.[^cite_note-212]

In a July 2021 joint statement with [NATO](21133.md), the individual [core Anglosphere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglosphere#Core_anglosphere)/[Five Eyes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Five_Eyes) nations, and Japan, the EU accused the Ministry of State Security, along with MSS-contracted criminal hackers, of perpetrating several cyberattacks, most notably the [2021 Microsoft Exchange Server data breach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Microsoft_Exchange_Server_data_breach). While some attacks were for-profit [ransomware](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ransomware) incidents by contracted hacker gangs, EU foreign policy commissioner [Josep Borrell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Josep_Borrell) said the hacking was "for the purpose of [intellectual property](14724.md) theft and [espionage](10501.md)."[^cite_note-19Jul1-213][^cite_note-19Jul2-214][^cite_note-19Jul3-215][^cite_note-19Jul4-216]

In March 2026, China was reported to have used fake [LinkedIn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LinkedIn) profiles to gather sensitive information from NATO and EU staff, targeting employees through fictitious recruitment accounts. The operation, attributed to the MSS, involved posing as recruiters to solicit paid reports and classified data.[^cite_note-217]

In July 2026, Belgian authorities arrested a Canadian former NATO intern on suspicion of espionage. [Reuters](18998750.md) and [BBC](19344654.md) later reported that Belgian investigators suspected the former NATO intern had been spying for China.[^cite_note-218][^cite_note-219]

#### Belgium

In 2008, [Belgian](3343.md) Justice Minister [Jo Vandeurzen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jo_Vandeurzen) accused the Chinese government of electronic espionage against the [government of Belgium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_of_Belgium), while [Foreign Minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Ministers_of_Foreign_Affairs_of_Belgium) [Karel De Gucht](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karel_De_Gucht) informed the [Belgian Federal Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belgian_Federal_Parliament) that Chinese agents hacked his ministry. The espionage is possibly linked to Belgium hosting the headquarters of NATO and the European Union.[^cite_note-220]

The [Katholieke Universiteit Leuven](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Katholieke_Universiteit_Leuven) in [Leuven](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leuven) was also believed to be the center for a group of Chinese students in Europe conducting industrial espionage, operating under a front organization called the Chinese Students' and Scholars' Association of Leuven.[^cite_note-221][^cite_note-222] In 2005 a leading figure of the Association defected to Belgium, providing information to the [Belgian State Security Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belgian_State_Security_Service) on hundreds of spies engaged in economic espionage across Europe.[^cite_note-223][^cite_note-224] The group had no obvious links to Chinese diplomats and was focused on getting [moles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mole_\(espionage\)) into laboratories and universities in the Netherlands, Britain, Germany, France and Belgium.[^cite_note-225] The *[People's Daily](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Daily)*, an organ of the [Central Committee of the Chinese Communist Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Committee_of_the_Chinese_Communist_Party), dismissed the reports as fabrications triggered by fears of China's economic development.[^cite_note-226]

In February 2019, a report was released by European External Action Service which estimated that there were 250 Chinese MSS spies operating in [Brussels](3708.md), the capital of the European Union.[^cite_note-227]

On 15 December 2023, a joint investigation by *[Financial Times](136566.md)*, *[Der Spiegel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Der_Spiegel)* and *Le Monde* revealed that former [Vlaams Belang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vlaams_Belang) senator [Frank Creyelman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frank_Creyelman) accepted bribes from Ministry of State Security for three years to influence discussions within the European Union.[^cite_note-228] Subsequently, Vlaams Belang expelled him from the party.[^cite_note-229] His brother, [Steven Creyelman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steven_Creyelman), also MP for the VB, is also linked to this case.[^cite_note-230]

In May 2026, Belgian authorities detained a senior researcher at semiconductor company BelGaN on suspicion of industrial espionage. The person is being held in prison for trial. Belgian prosecutors alleged that he transferred trade secrets concerning [gallium nitride](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gallium_nitride) semiconductor production to China.[^cite_note-231]

#### Denmark

In June 2023, [Bloomberg News](3057255.md) reported that [Huawei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Huawei) engaged in espionage against the [Danish telecom group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TDC_Holding_A/S) (TDC) based in Copenhagen, [Denmark](76972.md). This espionage aimed to gain an advantage in their bid against the Swedish telecommunications company [Ericsson](9424.md) for a lucrative 5G network contract valued at [CHF](361362.md) 175 million (\$198 million) to upgrade Denmark's cellular network.[^cite_note-232]

#### Estonia

In March 2021, Estonian scientist [Tarmo Kõuts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tarmo_K%C3%B5uts_\(marine_scientist\)) was convicted of spying for China. According to the [Estonian Internal Security Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_Internal_Security_Service), Kõuts was recruited in 2018 by China's Intelligence Bureau of the [Joint Staff Department of the Central Military Commission](49138480.md), and was arrested on 9 September 2020. Kõuts was paid approximately €17,000 by his handlers. Kõuts worked for the Maritime Institute of [Tallinn Technical University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tallinn_Technical_University), and was also a member of the Scientific Committee of the [Estonian Ministry of Defence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_of_Defence_\(Estonia\)) and the [NATO Undersea Research Centre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NATO_Undersea_Research_Centre).[^cite_note-DailyBeastMarch19-233]

#### Finland

According to the security research firm F5, Chinese hackers launched widespread attacks against Finnish [Internet of things](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internet_of_things) computers before the [2018 Russia–United States Summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_Russia%E2%80%93United_States_Summit) in [Helsinki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helsinki).[^cite_note-234][^cite_note-f5-235]

In March 2021, the Finnish government implicated the China-linked hacking group [APT31](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/APT31) in a hack of the [Finnish Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnish_Parliament).[^cite_note-236][^cite_note-237] In July that same year, as part of a joint [EU](9317.md)/NATO/[US](3434750.md)/[UK](31717.md) statement on Chinese-backed hacking campaigns (see 2021 Microsoft Exchange Server data breach), the British [National Cyber Security Centre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Cyber_Security_Centre_\(United_Kingdom\)) joined in accusing China-linked hacking groups of being behind the [Finnish Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament_of_Finland) hack.[^cite_note-19Jul2-214]

#### France

There have been several incidents of suspected Chinese spies in France. This includes [Shi Pei Pu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shi_Pei_Pu), a Chinese [opera singer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opera_singer) from Beijing who convinced a French diplomat that he was a woman and spied on France.[^cite_note-238]

French media also portrayed Li Li Whuang ([李李](https://zh.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E6%9D%8E%E6%9D%8E%E6%A1%88)), a 22-year-old Chinese intern at car parts maker [Valeo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Valeo), as an industrial spy.[^cite_note-239] Both the French prosecution and Valeo refuted media claims of spying and the case was later considered to be a psychosis.[^cite_note-240][^cite_note-241] Li Li was ultimately convicted of violating the confidentiality clause of her contract and served two months in prison, but was allowed to continue her doctoral studies at the [University of Technology of Compiègne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Technology_of_Compi%C3%A8gne).[^cite_note-242]

Two French intelligence operatives, identified only as Henri M and Pierre-Marie H, were accused of communicating classified information to China.[^cite_note-smhfrance-243] Henri M was reportedly the Beijing station chief for the [Directorate-General for External Security](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Directorate-General_for_External_Security).[^cite_note-smhfrance-243][^cite_note-244]

According to reporting by *[Le Figaro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Le_Figaro)*, the [General Directorate for Internal Security](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Directorate_for_Internal_Security) and Directorate-General for External Security believe that Chinese spies have used [LinkedIn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LinkedIn) to target thousands of business and government officials as potential sources of information.[^cite_note-245]

In 2011, a Chinese cyberattack against Airbus Astrium (now [ArianeGroup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ArianeGroup)) occurred, targeting technical specifications and test results of [M51 SLBM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M51_SLBM).[^cite_note-challenges2020-246]

In December 2018, experts from the DGSI and [ANSSI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ANSSI) discovered a "sophisticated" [supply chain attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supply_chain_attack) targeting [Airbus](26220236.md).[^cite_note-challenges2020-246] The pattern of the attack has been described as typical of [APT10](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/APT10) although unusually sophisticated.[^cite_note-247] It is suspected that the attack was motivated by [economic warfare](4757142.md) to benefit the development of the domestic Chinese [C919](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comac_C919) airliner.[^cite_note-challenges2020-246]

In 2019, the admiral Morio de l'Isle warned the [National Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assembl%C3%A9e_nationale) about a high number of marriages between Chinese women and French servicemen in [Brest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brest,_France), which host the general staff of the [Strategic Oceanic Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strategic_Oceanic_Force), responsible for the sea-based French nuclear deterrence, and located next to [Île Longue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%8Ele_Longue), the home base of French [SSBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SSBN). He also notified of an "increasingly strong" Chinese presence around [VLF](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VLF) military transmission center across France citing the example of the creation of "sino-european hub" funded by the investment company of Beijing Municipality in a remote town near the [HWU transmitter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HWU_transmitter). This choice was described by a local deputy as "troubling".[^cite_note-248][^cite_note-249]

In December 2020, Huawei announced it will open its first large factory outside China in France next to the border with Germany.[^cite_note-250] National and local newspapers have quickly expressed national security concerns about the location of the factory, situated between [DRM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Direction_du_renseignement_militaire) headquarters, multiple [electronic warfare](82272.md), intelligence and transmission regiments, as well as about [the company itself](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Criticism_of_Huawei#Espionage_and_security_concerns).[^cite_note-251][^cite_note-252][^cite_note-253][^cite_note-254] The US Consul of Strasbourg met with the local mayor.[^cite_note-255]

In July 2021, the ANSSI issued a security alert about a "large intrusion campaign impacting numerous French entities" described as "especially virulent" using the mode of operation of APT31.[^cite_note-256][^cite_note-257] Two weeks later, [Minister of the Armed Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_of_the_Armed_Forces_\(France\)) [Florence Parly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florence_Parly) at the [International Cybersecurity Forum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Cybersecurity_Forum?action=edit\&redlink=1) suggested the emergence of "a new cold war in cyberspace \[...] without the corresponding restraint. \[...] There is no [red phone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow%E2%80%93Washington_hotline) in the cyber" and warned "we could be confronted to quickly and uncontrollably escalating situations with unseen crisis and unpredictable domino effects".[^cite_note-258]

In March 2023, Taiwan's [Overseas Community Affairs Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Overseas_Community_Affairs_Council) (OCAC) announced that a Chinese overseas police station in France engaged in cyberattacks against an OCAC language school in France.[^cite_note-259]

A former employee of Beijing's Academy of Science and Technology and the president of Stahd Europe—a subsidiary of the Chinese telecommunications company Emposat—was reportedly involved in activities that raised national security concerns in France. French intelligence agents from the Defence Intelligence and Security Directorate ([DRSD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DRSD)) identified a suspicious satellite dish installed on a balcony near agricultural silos. Unlike standard television dishes, the equipment was outfitted with specialized components directed at the [CNES](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CNES) ([Centre National d'Études Spatiales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CNES)) ground stations in [Issus-Aussaguel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Issus_Aussaguel). These ground stations manage observation satellites developed by Airbus and Thales. The interception of signals from these satellites was considered a serious national security threat, prompting an investigation into possible espionage.[^cite_note-260]

In June 2026, *[Le Monde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Le_Monde)* reported that French counterintelligence services had broken up a network of nine Chinese police stations operating in France. French authorities said the sites operated under China's Ministry of Public Security and were used to monitor the Chinese diaspora, identify dissidents, collect intelligence, and recruit informants.[^cite_note-261]

#### Germany

According to reporting in *[Süddeutsche Zeitung](30873120.md)*, China has been soliciting information from members of the [Bundestag](3768.md), including offering €30,000 for insider information from one parliamentarian.[^cite_note-DWMP-262]

Between August and September 2007 Chinese hackers were suspected of using [Trojan horse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trojan_horse_\(computer\)) [spyware](28951.md) on various government computers, including those of the [Chancellory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chancellor_of_Germany_\(Federal_Republic_of_Germany\)), the [Ministry of Economics and Technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Ministry_of_Economics_and_Technology_\(Germany\)), and the [Ministry of Education and Research](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Ministry_of_Education_and_Research_\(Germany\)).[^cite_note-263] Germans officials believe Trojan viruses were inserted in Microsoft Word and [PowerPoint](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PowerPoint) files, and approximately 160 [gigabytes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gigabytes) of data were siphoned to [Guangzhou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guangzhou), [Lanzhou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lanzhou) and Beijing via South Korea, on instructions from the People's Liberation Army.[^cite_note-264]

The [Federal Ministry of the Interior](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Ministry_of_the_Interior_\(Germany\)) estimates that Chinese economic espionage could be costing Germany between 20 and 50 billion euros annually.[^cite_note-265] Spies are reportedly targeting mid- and small-scale companies that do not have as strong security regimens as larger corporations.[^cite_note-266] Berthold Stoppelkamp, head of the Working Group for Economic Security (ASW), stated that German companies had a poor security culture making espionage easier, exacerbated by the absence of a "strong, centralized" police command.[^cite_note-267] Walter Opfermann, a [counter-intelligence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Counter-intelligence) expert for the state of [Baden-Württemberg](66401.md), claimed that China is using extremely sophisticated electronic attacks capable of endangering portions of critical German infrastructure, having gathered sensitive information through techniques such as [phone hacking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phone_hacking) and Trojan emails.[^cite_note-268] In November 2018, German prosecutors in [Cologne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cologne) charged a former employee of [Lanxess](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lanxess) for engaging in industrial espionage on behalf of a Chinese copycat company.[^cite_note-269]

Germany suspects China of spying both on German corporations and on [Uyghur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uyghur_people) expatriates living in the country.[^cite_note-270][^cite_note-271] In 2011, a 64-year-old German man was charged with spying on Uyghurs in [Munich](19058.md) between April 2008 and October 2009.[^cite_note-272][^cite_note-273] Munich is a center for expatriate Uyghurs, and in November 2009 members of the [Federal Criminal Police Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Criminal_Police_Office_\(Germany\)) arrested four Chinese nationals on charges of spying on Uyghurs. In 2007 Chinese diplomat Ji Wumin left Germany after being observed meeting with individuals engaged in surveillance of Munich Uyghurs, and German investigators suspect China is coordinating espionage activities out of its Munich consulate in the [Neuhausen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neuhausen-Nymphenburg) district.[^cite_note-274]

In 2012, [EADS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EADS) (Airbus) and steel maker [ThyssenKrupp](944221.md) were attacked by Chinese hackers. ThyssenKrupp described the attack as "massive" and "especially qualitative".[^cite_note-275]

In 2016, ThyssenKrupp underwent an "organized, highly professional hacking attack" conducted "with state backing and the best attack techniques" whose aim was "to steal technological know-how and research".[^cite_note-handelsblatt2016-276] The group stated to have successfully repealed the attack after a "6 months long defensive battle". Some information was obtained by the hackers but critical divisions concerning blast furnace, power stations and [submarines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ThyssenKrupp_Marine_Systems) have reportedly not been affected.[^cite_note-277] The timing of the attacks suggest that attackers are based in China or southeast Asia.[^cite_note-handelsblatt2016-276]

In 2017, the [Federal Office for the Protection of the Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Office_for_the_Protection_of_the_Constitution) (BfV) published information alleging that Chinese intelligence services had created fake [social media](5897742.md) profiles on sites such as LinkedIn, using them to gather information on German politicians and government officials.[^cite_note-278][^cite_note-279] The [Verfassungsschutz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Verfassungsschutz) had previously warned that Chinese intelligence officers are making use of [social networking sites](2041117.md) such as LinkedIn and [XING](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/XING) to recruit informants.[^cite_note-280] Lu Kang of the Ministry of Foreign Affairs denied the allegations.[^cite_note-281]

In 2019, an investigation led by the [Bayerischer Rundfunk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bayerischer_Rundfunk) uncovered that a hacker group called [Winnti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Winnti) had attacked twelve major companies including six German [DAX](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DAX)-traded companies: [Bayer AG](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bayer_AG), [BASF](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BASF), [Siemens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siemens), [Henkel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henkel), [Covestro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Covestro).[^cite_note-br2019-282] The Kapersky Lab first uncovered Winnti in 2011 after it infected the German company Gameforge and has been highly active ever since. An IT security expert with whom they have worked said that "any DAX corporation that hasn't been attacked by Winnti must have done something wrong".[^cite_note-br2019-282] This group has also been attributed the responsibility of the 2016 ThyssenKrupp hacking attack.[^cite_note-283]

> We can, based on many, many indicators, say with high confidence that Winnti is being directed by the Chinese \[government].

— Dror-John Röcher (German Cyber Security Organization \[DCSO])[^cite_note-br2019-282]

In October 2024, Germany arrests Chinese woman Yaqi X for passing defense info to China via EU lawmaker [Maximilian Krah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maximilian_Krah)’s Chinese aide Jian Guo.[^cite_note-284]

In January 2025, German prosecutors indicted three individuals suspected of spying for China by gathering information on military technology for China's Ministry of State Security. The German Federal Criminal Police Office (BKA) arrested Herwig F., Ina F. and Thomas R., in [Düsseldorf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/D%C3%BCsseldorf) and [Bad Homburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bad_Homburg) in April 2024.[^cite_note-285]

In April 2025, a Chinese man, Jian Guo, who worked for a prominent German far-right lawmaker [Maximilian Krah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maximilian_Krah) in the European Parliament has been charged with spying for China for more than four years.[^cite_note-286]

In May 2026, German police arrested a married couple named Xuejun C. and Hua S. in Munich accused of spying for China by collecting sensitive information on aerospace, AI, and other technologies with possible military use. Prosecutors said the pair built contacts with German researchers and universities on behalf of Chinese intelligence.[^cite_note-287][^cite_note-288][^cite_note-289]

#### Lithuania

Lithuanian intelligence agencies have claimed that China is engaged in an "increasingly aggressive" campaign of espionage, which includes "attempts to recruit Lithuanian citizens". Darius Jauniskis, Director of the [State Security Department of Lithuania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_Security_Department_of_Lithuania), has cautioned against a potential threat posed by Huawei telecommunications equipment.[^cite_note-SCMPFeb9-290]

#### Norway

Hackers working as part of APT 10, on behalf of the Chinese government, hacked Norwegian business software provider [Visma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Visma), reportedly to gain access to the information on the company's customers.[^cite_note-291] Beginning on 30 August 2018, APT10 used a malware program dubbed Trochilus and accessed a [backdoor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Backdoor_\(computing\)), and then proceeded to use [WinRAR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WinRAR) and [cURL](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CURL) to exfiltrate data from Visma to a [Dropbox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dropbox_\(service\)) account.[^cite_note-Insikt-292] Norwegian parliamentary email accounts were breached by Chinese state hackers during the [2021 Microsoft Exchange Server data breach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Microsoft_Exchange_Server_data_breach).[^cite_note-293][^cite_note-294]

In July 2024, Norwegian authorities arrested a suspected spy for China.[^cite_note-295] In May 2026, the [Norwegian Police Security Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Norwegian_Police_Security_Service) arrested a Chinese national on suspicion of attempting to illegally obtain satellite data.[^cite_note-296][^cite_note-297]

#### Poland

On 19 April 2009, Stefan Zielonka, a [non-commissioned officer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-commissioned_officer) serving as a cipher operator for the [Military Information Services](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_Information_Services_\(Poland\)) and later the [Military Intelligence Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_Intelligence_Service_\(Poland\)?action=edit\&redlink=1), mysteriously disappeared. Following his disappearance, some speculated that Zielonka may have worked for Russian intelligence or defected to China, where his expertise in [cryptography](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cryptography) and knowledge of [NATO communication systems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NATO_Communications_and_Information_Agency) could have provided valuable information.[^cite_note-298][^cite_note-299] Conversely, others suggested that Zielonka struggled with depression and personal issues, though this was disputed by some. On 27 April 2010, Zielonka's body was found in an advanced state of decomposition on the banks of the [Vistula River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vistula), with a rope hanging from a nearby tree and documents bearing his name. Some speculated that his [death may have been staged](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Faked_death). Eventually, [DNA testing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DNA_profiling) of four bone fragments confirmed the body was his, and investigators ruled out third-party involvement.[^cite_note-300] However, the case and its investigation remained shrouded in numerous unresolved and puzzling circumstances.[^cite_note-301]

In April 2018, a former member of the [Parliament of Poland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament_of_Poland) for [Samoobrona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samoobrona), Mateusz Piskorski, was charged with espionage on behalf of Russia and China.[^cite_note-302][^cite_note-303]

In January 2019, the Huawei sales director for Poland, identified as Weijing Wang (a.k.a. "Stanislaw Wang") was arrested, along with a former senior agent of the [Agencja Bezpieczeństwa Wewnętrznego](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agencja_Bezpiecze%C5%84stwa_Wewn%C4%99trznego) (ISA) named Piotr Durbajlo, on suspicion of espionage.[^cite_note-SCMPJan11-304][^cite_note-305] Wang was educated at the [Beijing Foreign Studies University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beijing_Foreign_Studies_University) and studied [Polish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_language) in [Łódź](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C5%81%C3%B3d%C5%BA), and subsequently worked as a [cultural attaché](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_attach%C3%A9) at the Chinese consulate in [Gdańsk](12099.md).[^cite_note-306][^cite_note-ReutersJuly2-307] Wang joined Huawei's Enterprise Business Group in 2017.
